It just sort of… happened.\n\nDoctor: When you eat less, is it mostly because you just don’t feel hungry?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think… mostly, yeah. My scrubs are loose now, which is weird because I’m not trying to lose weight or anything. It just kind of slipped away from me.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding food because you’re worried about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: No, not like… I’m not avoiding it because I’m worried I’ll gain weight. It’s more that food just kind of falls off the list. I’ll have coffee and maybe a granola bar before my shift, and then once I’m at work in my scrubs, moving around, answering people, doing the usual stuff, I don’t really stop.\n\nThen I notice later my scrubs are hanging loose, which bothers me because I’m not trying to lose weight. It just sort of happened.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after something stressful happened in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it kind of did. Not like one big dramatic thing, but when the clinic changed the EMR and redid all the workflow stuff, my hours shifted around and everything got more… I don’t know, chopped up.\n\nThat’s when it started, I think. It’s been going on since then—almost two years now, which sounds longer when I say it out loud. The whole schedule got weird, and then eating just sort of stopped being a normal part of the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most days?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… anxious most days? I’m not sure I’d call it that. Some days I feel keyed up, I guess, but then other days I’m just trying to get through the shift and the little stuff, like whether I packed something or where I put my badge.\n\nI mean, work can make anybody tense, right? Especially when people are waiting on you and the computer’s doing whatever it wants. But “most days”… I’d have to think about that. I haven’t really been tracking it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have. I used to run over in Van Cortlandt Park, not like serious marathon stuff, just enough to clear my head. And I’d take photos sometimes, little things, the trees or people playing baseball, whatever caught my eye.\n\nI used to play chess with friends too, but lately when I think about doing any of that, I just kind of tell myself, “What’s the point?” Then I end up sitting at home, scrolling on my phone or checking my budget spreadsheet again, which is not exactly a hobby.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, yeah. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Um… yeah, hi. I guess the main thing is my eating’s gotten really off lately. Like, some days before work I only manage coffee and maybe a granola bar, and then I’m running around in my scrubs all shift—well, not running exactly, but you know how it is in healthcare support, there’s always something—and I realized my scrubs are kind of hanging loose on me now.\n\nI’m not trying to lose weight or anything. It just sort of… happened.\n\nDoctor: When you eat less, is it mostly because you just don’t feel hungry?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think… mostly, yeah. It’s like I don’t really feel hungry, or I don’t notice it until later. I’ll have coffee and maybe a granola bar before work, and then once I’m in my scrubs and the shift starts, I just kind of keep going.\n\nAnd then I get home and realize, oh, I didn’t really eat. My scrubs are loose now, which is weird because I’m not trying to lose weight or anything. It just kind of slipped away from me.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding food because you’re worried about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: No, not like… I’m not avoiding it because I’m worried I’ll gain weight. It’s more that food just kind of falls off the list. I’ll have coffee and maybe a granola bar before my shift, and then once I’m at work in my scrubs, moving around, answering people, doing the usual stuff, I don’t really stop.\n\nThen I notice later my scrubs are hanging loose, which bothers me because I’m not trying to lose weight. It just sort of happened.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after something stressful happened in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it kind of did. Not like one big dramatic thing, but when the clinic changed the EMR and redid all the workflow stuff, my hours shifted around and everything got more… I don’t know, chopped up.\n\nThat’s when it started, I think. It’s been going on since then—almost two years now, which sounds longer when I say it out loud. The whole schedule got weird, and then eating just sort of stopped being a normal part of the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most days?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… anxious most days? I’m not sure I’d call it that. Some days I feel keyed up, I guess, but then other days I’m just trying to get through the shift and the little stuff, like whether I packed something or where I put my badge.\n\nI mean, work can make anybody tense, right? Especially when people are waiting on you and the computer’s doing whatever it wants. But “most days”… I’d have to think about that. I haven’t really been tracking it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have. I used to run over in Van Cortlandt Park, not like serious marathon stuff, just enough to clear my head. And I’d take photos sometimes, little things, the trees or people playing baseball, whatever caught my eye.\n\nI used to play chess with friends too, but lately when I think about doing any of that, I just kind of tell myself, “What’s the point?” Then I end up sitting at home, scrolling on my phone or checking my budget spreadsheet again, which is not exactly a hobby.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, yeah. Mornings are the worst, I think. I wake up and it’s like this heavy, hollow feeling is already sitting there, like the day’s ruined before I even get up, before I even put on my badge.\n\nAnd then I’m thinking about stupid stuff too, like whether the badge reel is in my jacket or if I left it by the sink, because I did that once and had to go all the way back—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. But yeah, down. That’s probably the word.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I… yeah, sometimes. Not like I’ve made a plan or anything like that. I haven’t sat down and thought, “I’m going to do this.” It’s more like… I’ll wake up and think it would’ve been easier if I just didn’t wake up at all.\n\nOr I catch myself thinking maybe if I disappeared, people wouldn’t have to deal with me. Then I’ll start thinking about bills or whether I left something at work, and I kind of shove it aside. But yeah… those thoughts come up.\n\nDoctor: How has your sleep been lately?\n\nPatient: Not great. I’ll get into bed and I’m tired, but then I’m just lying there replaying stuff from work, like what I should’ve done differently or whether I forgot something. And I start listening for noises too, like every little sound in the hallway or outside, which is ridiculous, but I still do it.\n\nThen I’ll finally sleep some, but I keep waking up around 3 or 4 in the morning. After that I can’t really settle back down before the alarm goes off, so I’m just kind of waiting for the day to start.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. It’s not always like I can’t read or anything, but I lose the thread really fast.\n\nLike at work, someone will ask me a simple intake question, just one thing, and I’ll start answering but then I’m explaining what screen it is in the EMR, and then I’m talking about how short we were staffed that day, and then somehow I’m bringing up something I read for licensure… and I can see on their face that I’m not answering what they asked anymore.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly. I know there was a point, but I kind of drift away from it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or like you’re a burden to other people?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I keep feeling like I’m just making things harder for everybody. Like at the clinic, if I’m a little slow turning over a room or I miss some small thing, I’ll go home thinking I ruined the whole flow of the day for everyone.\n\nAnd with my family too… I start thinking they’d be better off if they didn’t have to worry about me. It sounds dramatic when I say it, because it might just be one little delay or one mistake, but in my head it turns into this big thing, like I’ve burdened everybody.\n\nDoctor: For at least two weeks, have you felt down like this nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it’s been pretty much every day. Most mornings I wake up with this heavy, hollow feeling, like the day is already ruined before I even put on my badge.\n\nAnd then I’ll start thinking about getting to work, the parking, who’s on the schedule, whether I’m going to mess something up… but the feeling is already there before any of that. I’ll have coffee and maybe a granola bar before my shift, and then once I’m at work in my scrubs, moving around, answering people, doing the usual stuff, I don’t really stop.\n\nThen I notice later my scrubs are hanging loose, which bothers me because I’m not trying to lose weight. It just sort of happened.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after something stressful happened in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it kind of did. Not like one big dramatic thing, but when the clinic changed the EMR and redid all the workflow stuff, my hours shifted around and everything got more… I don’t know, chopped up.\n\nThat’s when it started, I think. It’s been going on since then—almost two years now, which sounds longer when I say it out loud. The whole schedule got weird, and then eating just sort of stopped being a normal part of the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most days?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… anxious most days? I’m not sure I’d call it that. I haven’t really been tracking it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have. I used to run over in Van Cortlandt Park, not like serious marathon stuff, just enough to clear my head. Mornings are the worst, I think. I wake up and it’s like this heavy, hollow feeling is already sitting there, like the day’s ruined before I even get up, before I even put on my badge.\n\nAnd then I’m thinking about stupid stuff too, like whether the badge reel is in my jacket or if I left it by the sink, because I did that once and had to go all the way back—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. But yeah, down. That’s probably the word.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I… yeah, sometimes. Not like I’ve made a plan or anything like that. I haven’t sat down and thought, “I’m going to do this.” It’s more like… I’ll wake up and think it would’ve been easier if I just didn’t wake up at all.\n\nOr I catch myself thinking maybe if I disappeared, people wouldn’t have to deal with me. Then I’ll start thinking about bills or whether I left something at work, and I kind of shove it aside. But yeah… those thoughts come up.\n\nDoctor: How has your sleep been lately?\n\nPatient: Not great. I’ll get into bed and I’m tired, but then I’m just lying there replaying stuff from work, like what I should’ve done differently or whether I forgot something. And I start listening for noises too, like every little sound in the hallway or outside, which is ridiculous, but I still do it.\n\nThen I’ll finally sleep some, but I keep waking up around 3 or 4 in the morning. After that I can’t really settle back down before the alarm goes off, so I’m just kind of waiting for the day to start.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. It’s not always like I can’t read or anything, but I lose the thread really fast.\n\nLike at work, someone will ask me a simple intake question, just one thing, and I’ll start answering but then I’m explaining what screen it is in the EMR, and then I’m talking about how short we were staffed that day, and then somehow I’m bringing up something I read for licensure… and I can see on their face that I’m not answering what they asked anymore.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly. I know there was a point, but I kind of drift away from it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or like you’re a burden to other people?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I keep feeling like I’m just making things harder for everybody. Like at the clinic, if I’m a little slow turning over a room or I miss some small thing, I’ll go home thinking I ruined the whole flow of the day for everyone.\n\nAnd with my family too… I start thinking they’d be better off if they didn’t have to worry about me. It sounds dramatic when I say it, because it might just be one little delay or one mistake, but in my head it turns into this big thing, like I’ve burdened everybody.\n\nDoctor: For at least two weeks, have you felt down like this nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it’s been pretty much every day. Most mornings I wake up with this heavy, hollow feeling, like the day is already ruined before I even put on my badge.\n\nAnd then I’ll start thinking about getting to work, the parking, who’s on the schedule, whether I’m going to mess something up… but the feeling is already there before any of that. It’s kind of one or the other.\n\nAt work I’ll catch myself pacing around the supply area, opening the same drawers over and over like I’m checking for something, even though I already looked. And then other times I’m just… slow. Like drawing up labels, answering a patient, stuff that should be quick, it takes me twice as long.\n\nAnd then I start thinking about how the drawers are never stocked the same way depending on who closed the night before, which probably isn’t even the point… but yeah, people can probably notice I’m either restless or moving really slowly.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I have. And I start listening for noises too, like every little sound in the hallway or outside, which is ridiculous, but I still do it.\n\nThen I’ll finally sleep some, but I keep waking up around 3 or 4 in the morning. After that I can’t really settle back down before the alarm goes off, so I’m just kind of waiting for the day to start.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. It’s not always like I can’t read or anything, but I lose the thread really fast.\n\nLike at work, someone will ask me a simple intake question, just one thing, and I’ll start answering but then I’m explaining what screen it is in the EMR, and then I’m talking about how short we were staffed that day, and then somehow I’m bringing up something I read for licensure… and I can see on their face that I’m not answering what they asked anymore.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly. I know there was a point, but I kind of drift away from it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or like you’re a burden to other people?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. It sounds dramatic when I say it, because it might just be one little delay or one mistake, but in my head it turns into this big thing, like I’ve burdened everybody.\n\nDoctor: For at least two weeks, have you felt down like this nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it’s been pretty much every day. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Before it really settled in? Yeah, I think there was… I mean, I wasn’t walking around thrilled or anything, but there were times I was mostly okay emotionally.

The steady low mood and not really caring about things, that came later. It’s been going on now for, I guess, about a year and four months. I always try to line it up with work schedules, because that’s how I remember time—like which unit I was floated to, who was on nights—but yeah, roughly sixteen months where it’s been pretty constant.

I know there was a point, but I kind of drift away from it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or like you’re a burden to other people?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do.     "psychosocial_stressor": true,
    "additional_requirements": [
      "Appetite loss and weight loss were confirmed to be undesired and not motivated by fear of weight gain or body-image concerns.",
      "Eating reduction was explicitly linked by the patient to depressed mood.",
      "No suicide plan or intent was reported, though passive death wishes were present."
    ]
  }
}
